=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Given a string s of length n and an integer k, determine whether it is possible to select k disjoint special substrings.
A special substring is a substring where:

Any character present inside the substring should not appear outside it in the string.
The substring is not the entire string s.

Note that all k substrings must be disjoint, meaning they cannot overlap.
Return true if it is possible to select k such disjoint special substrings; otherwise, return false.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "abcdbaefab", k = 2
Output: true
Explanation:

We can select two disjoint special substrings: "cd" and "ef".
"cd" contains the characters 'c' and 'd', which do not appear elsewhere in s.
"ef" contains the characters 'e' and 'f', which do not appear elsewhere in s.


Example 2:

Input: s = "cdefdc", k = 3
Output: false
Explanation:
There can be at most 2 disjoint special substrings: "e" and "f". Since k = 3, the output is false.

Example 3:

Input: s = "abeabe", k = 0
Output: true

 
Constraints:

2 <= n == s.length <= 5 * 10^4
0 <= k <= 26
s consists only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxSubstringLength(self, s: str, k: int) -> bool:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

A minimal special interval [l,r] is special and contains no proper special subinterval. What does it look like? If there is any position i inside such that singleton? Not necessarily. Let [l,r] be special. It is a union of complete color classes. If there is a proper subinterval [l',r'] that is also a union of complete color classes and contiguous, then it's special. Since [l,r] is union of color classes. A proper subinterval is special iff the set of colors appearing in it has union exactly [l',r'] (contiguous) and no partial colors. This can happen if there is a gap? Actually [l',r'] is contiguous by definition; it contains some colors C'. For it to be special, every color in C' must have all occurrences inside [l',r']; i.e., no color occurrence of C' outside [l',r'] but inside [l,r]. Since [l,r] special, colors in C' have all occurrences inside [l,r], but may extend beyond [l',r'] within [l,r]. Thus [l',r'] special iff for every color appearing in [l',r'], its first and last occurrences are within [l',r']. In other words, [l',r'] is a "closed interval" in the induced string s[l..r].

Thus minimal special intervals are intervals that are closed and have no proper closed subinterval. This is analogous to "prime" intervals in a string under closure operator.

Thus algorithm:
1. n = len(s)
2. Compute first_pos[26], last_pos[26].

       # If yes and not (l==0 and R==n-1), add interval (l,R).

Simplest robust:
For each candidate l:
    R = l
    while True:
        # compute max_last and min_first over s[l:R+1]
        max_last = 0; min_first = n
        for i in range(l, R+1):
            c = s[i]
            if first_pos[c] < min_first: min_first = first_pos[c]
            if last_pos[c] > max_last: max_last = last_pos[c]
        if max_last == R:
            break
        R = max_last
    # check min_first == l and not (l==0 and R==n-1)

Let's prove more rigorously.

Let F be set of all special intervals. For each I=[l,r] in F, let C(I)=closure starting at l, which is in our representative set R and C(I) ⊆ I. We want max number of disjoint intervals from F. Claim max over F equals max over R? 
- Any selection from R is selection from F, so max_F >= max_R.
- Take optimal selection S from F. Replace each I in S by C(I). Since C(I) ⊆ I and I's are disjoint, C(I) are disjoint. Thus we get a selection from R of same size. So max_R >= max_F. Thus equality. Thus algorithm with at most 26 candidates is extremely simple. Need implement closure efficiently.

Implementation details:

- Use ord(c)-97.
- first = [-1]*26, last = [-1]*26
- For i,ch in enumerate(s):
    c=ord(ch)-97
    if first[c]==-1: first[c]=i
    last[c]=i
- candidates = [i for i in range(n) if first[ord(s[i])-97] == i]  # at most 26.
- intervals = []
- For l in candidates:
    R = l
    valid = True
    while True:
        mn = n
        mx = -1
        # scan positions l..R
        for i in range(l, R+1):
            c = ord(s[i])-97
            if first[c] < mn: mn = first[c]
            if last[c] > mx: mx = last[c]
        if mn < l:
            valid = False
            break
        if mx == R:
            break
        # mx > R
        R = mx
        # optional: if R >= n? no
    if valid and not (l == 0 and R == n-1):
        intervals.append((R, l))  # sort by end
- Sort intervals by R ascending (we store (R,l)).
- Greedy:
    count=0; last_end=-1
    for R,l in sorted(intervals):
        if l > last_end:
            count += 1
            last_end = R
- Return count >= k.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def maxSubstringLength(self, s: str, k: int) -> bool:
        n = len(s)
        first = [-1]*26
        last = [-1]*26
        for i,ch in enumerate(s):
            c = ord(ch)-97
            if first[c] == -1:
                first[c] = i
            last[c] = i
        
        intervals = []
        for l in range(n):
            if first[ord(s[l])-97] != l:
                continue
            R = l
            ok = True
            while True:
                mn = n
                mx = -1
                # scan l..R
                for i in range(l, R+1):
                    c = ord(s[i])-97
                    fc = first[c]
                    if fc < mn:
                        mn = fc
                    lc = last[c]
                    if lc > mx:
                        mx = lc
                if mn < l:
                    ok = False
                    break
                if mx == R:
                    break
                # mx > R
                R = mx
            if ok and not (l == 0 and R == n-1):
                intervals.append((R, l))
        
        intervals.sort()
        cnt = 0
        last_end = -1
        for R, l in intervals:
            if l > last_end:
                cnt += 1
                last_end = R
        return cnt >= k

Now, let's think of optimizing scanning. Since at most 26 candidates, but n up to 5e4, scanning [l,R] from scratch each expansion could in worst-case be 26 candidates * 26 expansions * 50000 = 33.8M. But we can improve by maintaining a bitmask of chars in current interval and updating as R expands. Let's consider for cleaner and faster.

For each candidate l:
    R = l
    mask = 0  # chars seen in [l,R]
    # We can initialize by scanning from l to l? But R may jump; we can maintain a pointer p = l-1 representing last position added.
    # Each time R increases, add positions p+1..R to mask.
    # Then compute mn and mx from mask? Need min first and max last among chars in mask. Since alphabet 26, we can compute by iterating over bits in mask (<=26) each iteration, not scanning string. This is faster and avoids repeated scanning.
    p = l - 1
    R = l
    while True:
        # expand to R
        while p < R:
            p += 1
            c = ord(s[p])-97
            mask |= 1 << c
        # compute mn, mx from mask
        mn = n; mx = -1
        m = mask
        while m:
            lb = m & -m
            c = lb.bit_length()-1
            if first[c] < mn: mn = first[c]
            if last[c] > mx: mx = last[c]
            m -= lb
        if mn < l:
            ok=False; break
        if mx == R:
            break
        R = mx
    ...
This scans each position in final interval at most once per candidate, so O(n*26) worst 1.3M, plus bit operations. Better. Need be careful: When R jumps, we add positions up to new R. But if later R increases further, p already at old R, add new positions. Good. The mask includes chars in [l,R] because we added all positions from l to current R. We don't remove chars (interval only expands, no left movement). Good.

Then compute mn/mx from mask. Since alphabet 26, bit iteration okay. We can also precompute for each mask? Not needed.

This is cleaner and avoids repeated scanning. Let's implement this.

Pseudo:

intervals = []
for l in range(n):
    if first[ord(s[l])-97] != l:
        continue
    R = l
    p = l - 1
    mask = 0
    ok = True
    while True:
        while p < R:
            p += 1
            c = ord(s[p]) - 97
            mask |= 1 << c
        mn = n
        mx = -1
        m = mask
        while m:
            lsb = m & -m
            c = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            if first[c] < mn:
                mn = first[c]
            if last[c] > mx:
                mx = last[c]
            m -= lsb
        if mn < l:
            ok = False
            break
        if mx == R:
            break
        R = mx
    if ok and not (l == 0 and R == n - 1):
        intervals.append((R, l))

Need ensure if R jumps to mx, and mx may be less than p? mx > previous R = p? At start p = l-1, R=l. After computing mx, if mx>R, set R=mx. Then while p<R adds positions. Good.

But wait, mask includes chars in [l,R] after expansion. However, when we compute mn/mx, we use mask. But what if R is set to mx, and mx is a position that was already added? mx > previous R, so not added yet. Good.

Now, closure expansion count still <=26? With mask, the while loop could run more than 26? Each iteration after computing mx, if mx>R, R increases to last_pos of some char in mask. The mask grows; each time R becomes last_pos[c] for some char c. Could the same char cause expansion again? Suppose c in mask, last_pos[c] = mx > R, so R becomes last_pos[c]. Later, c remains in mask, but last_pos[c] <= R, so c cannot be mx > R. Thus each char at most once. So <=26 iterations. Good.

Now, if mn<l, we abort. But note: We compute mn/mx from mask after expanding to R. If mn<l, invalid. We break. No need to continue. Good.

Now, one subtlety: The closure process should compute max_last over [l,R] after expanding. But if mn<l, we abort before checking mx==R. Suppose mn<l and mx==R, interval invalid; no special. If mn<l and mx>R, also invalid. Good.

Now, what if mn<l but there is a smaller R' < current R where mn>=l and mx==R'? Could closure have stopped earlier? Let's see. We only expand R when previous mx > R. At previous iteration, R_old, mx_old > R_old. So [l,R_old] not special. We set R=mx_old. If there was a special [l,r] with R_old < r < mx_old, then the char causing mx_old (last_pos=mx_old) appears in [l,R_old] and has occurrence after r, so [l,r] not special. Thus no. So abort okay.

Code:

class Solution:
    def maxSubstringLength(self, s: str, k: int) -> bool:
        n = len(s)
        a = [ord(ch) - 97 for ch in s]
        first = [-1] * 26
        last = [-1] * 26
        for i, c in enumerate(a):
            if first[c] == -1:
                first[c] = i
            last[c] = i
        
        intervals = []
        for l in range(n):
            if first[a[l]] != l:
                continue
            R = l
            p = l - 1
            mask = 0
            ok = True
            while True:
                while p < R:
                    p += 1
                    mask |= 1 << a[p]
                mn = n
                mx = -1
                m = mask
                while m:
                    lsb = m & -m
                    c = lsb.bit_length() - 1
                    if first[c] < mn:
                        mn = first[c]
                    if last[c] > mx:
                        mx = last[c]
                    m -= lsb
                if mn < l:
                    ok = False
                    break
                if mx == R:
                    break
                R = mx
            if ok and not (l == 0 and R == n - 1):
                intervals.append((R, l))
        
        intervals.sort()
        cnt = 0
        last_end = -1
        for R, l in intervals:
            if l > last_end:
                cnt += 1
                last_end = R
        return cnt >= k

Need check if mask includes chars from l to R after R jumps. Suppose R can jump to mx, but mx might be less than p? No, mx > previous R = p? Wait after expansion, p == R. Then compute mx. If mx > R, set R=mx. Then while p<R adds positions. Good.

Now, one subtle issue: We compute mn/mx from mask after expanding. But if R jumps to mx, and mx is a position whose char was already in mask? Actually mx is last_pos of some char in mask, but the position mx itself may not have been added yet (since mx > previous R). It contains that char, so adding it doesn't change mask. But we still need to add positions between previous R+1 and mx, which may include new chars. Good.

Now, what if R jumps to mx, but mx is n? last_pos max n-1. Good.

Now, let's think if the closure process with mask and abort on mn<l might miss a valid interval if mn<l at an intermediate R but later R expands to include the earlier occurrence of that char? Wait earlier occurrence is before l, cannot be included because interval starts at l. So no.

Now, let's consider if a char with first<l appears in [l,R], but its first occurrence before l is actually not "outside" because it's in a different selected substring? No, for a substring to be special, any occurrence outside the substring violates, regardless of selection. So invalid.

Now, let's think about the proof that closure expansions <=26 with mask. Suppose mask contains char c with last_pos[c] = mx > R. We set R=mx. Later, could c again have last_pos > R? No, R >= last_pos[c]. So each char can be the source of expansion at most once. Since 26 chars, loop <=27 iterations. Good.

Now, let's consider if we should include intervals where R < l? no.

Wait if [l,R'] special, then max_last(l,R') = R'. So if closure process at some R_current < R' computes max_last(l,R_current) = mx. If mx > R_current, it sets R=mx. Could mx > R'? If mx > R', then there is a char in [l,R_current] with last_pos mx > R'. Since [l,R_current] subset [l,R'], that char appears in [l,R'] and has occurrence after R', contradicting [l,R'] special. Thus mx <= R'. So closure never jumps past R'. Thus if closure final R > R', impossible. Good.
